--- PAGE 1 ---
The  Imprisoned  Gods  of  Belavadös  
The  beings  known  as  The  Gods  are  not  divine  in  any  benevolent  sense  of  the  word.  They  are  
not
 
protectors,
 
moral
 
guides,
 
or
 
loving
 
creators.
 
They
 
are
 
ancient
 
predators
 
who
 
shaped
 
the
 
peoples
 
of
 
Belavadös
 
from
 
their
 
own
 
designs,
 
cultivating
 
mortal
 
life
 
not
 
out
 
of
 
compassion,
 
but
 
fascination,
 
utility,
 
and
 
hunger.
 
Every
 
race
 
in
 
the
 
world
 
traces
 
its
 
origin
 
to
 
one
 
of
 
these
 
entities.
 
Humanity
 
and
 
all
 
its
 
neighboring
 
bloodlines,
 
the
 
elves,
 
dwarves,
 
beastfolk,
 
dragons,
 
constructs,
 
shadow-born
 
peoples,
 
and
 
stranger
 
things
 
still
 
all
 
exist
 
because
 
the
 
Gods
 
desired
 
them
 
to.
 
For  most  of  history,  the  Gods  ruled  openly.  
Their  reign  ended  only  a  few  generations  ago.  
There  are  still  elders  alive  who  remember  when  the  Gods  walked  freely  through  cities.  There  are  
rulers
 
whose
 
grandparents
 
once
 
knelt
 
before
 
them
 
in
 
person.
 
Ancient
 
temples
 
still
 
stand
 
in
 
active
 
use.
 
Some
 
noble
 
bloodlines
 
carry
 
marks
 
and
 
blessings
 
granted
 
directly
 
by
 
the
 
Gods
 
themselves.
 
The
 
scars
 
of
 
divine
 
rule
 
are
 
recent
 
enough
 
that
 
the
 
world
 
has
 
not
 
fully
 
recovered
 
from
 
it.
 
The  imprisonment  of  the  Gods  was  not  born  from  heroism  alone.  
The  great  courts,  empires,  magocracies,  and  royal  houses  of  the  world  eventually  united  against  
them
 
after
 
centuries
 
of
 
exploitation,
 
feeding,
 
wars,
 
and
 
manipulation.
 
Entire
 
civilizations
 
had
 
become
 
dependent
 
upon
 
the
 
Gods’
 
touch
 
and
 
the
 
substances
 
derived
 
from
 
them.
 
Kingdoms
 
rose
 
and
 
collapsed
 
according
 
to
 
divine
 
appetites.
 
The
 
Gods
 
cultivated
 
worship
 
through
 
addiction,
 
fear,
 
ecstasy,
 
and
 
dependency
 
until
 
much
 
of
 
the
 
world
 
could
 
no
 
longer
 
distinguish
 
faith
 
from
 
enslavement.
 
Yet  even  when  the  rulers  of  Belavadös  rebelled,  they  did  not  destroy  the  Gods.  
They  could  have  tried.  
Instead,  they  chose  imprisonment.  
The  decision  was  deliberate,  political,  and  deeply  corrupt.  
The  Gods  were  too  useful.  
Their  blood,  called  ichor,  had  become  woven  into  every  powerful  institution  on  the  continent.  
Ichor
 
could
 
heal
 
disease,
 
strengthen
 
magic,
 
extend
 
life,
 
fuel
 
rituals,
 
empower
 
curses,
 
create
 
poisons,
 
alter
 
minds,
 
and
 
sustain
 
entire
 
industries
 
of
 
alchemy
 
and
 
sorcery.
 
Fresh
 
ichor
 
could
 
be
 
consumed
 
directly
 
for
 
euphoric
 
visions
 
and
 
temporary
 
supernatural
 
gifts.
 
When
 
dried
 
and
 
refined
 
into
 
black
 
crystalline
 
powder,
 
it
 
became
 
one
 
of
 
the
 
most
 
valuable
 
substances
 
in
 
existence.
 
--- PAGE 2 ---
Entire  economies  depended  upon  it.  
The  rulers  of  the  world  did  not  wish  to  free  their  people  from  the  Gods  completely.  They  wanted  
control
 
over
 
them.
 
So  the  great  prisons  were  built.  
Across  Belavadös,  hidden  beneath  palaces,  oceans,  mountains,  cathedrals,  and  sealed  
vault-cities,
 
the
 
Gods
 
remain
 
confined
 
within
 
colossal
 
arcane
 
prisons
 
maintained
 
by
 
generations
 
of
 
royal
 
bloodlines,
 
mage
 
orders,
 
and
 
secretive
 
courts.
 
These
 
prisons
 
are
 
not
 
simple
 
cages.
 
They
 
are
 
feeding
 
engines
 
and
 
suppression
 
systems
 
designed
 
specifically
 
to
 
keep
 
the
 
Gods
 
alive,
 
weakened,
 
and
 
harvestable.
 
The  prisons  force  limitations  upon  them.  
The  Gods  are  still  allowed  to  feed,  because  starvation  would  eventually  drive  them  feral  and  
uncontrollable.
 
However,
 
the
 
enchantments
 
binding
 
them
 
prevent
 
them
 
from
 
fully
 
killing
 
during
 
the
 
feeding
 
process.
 
The
 
prisons
 
interrupt
 
the
 
final
 
stages
 
of
 
consumption,
 
forcibly
 
cutting
 
the
 
Gods
 
off
 
before
 
death
 
can
 
occur.
 
This
 
leaves
 
them
 
trapped
 
in
 
a
 
permanent
 
state
 
of
 
weakness
 
and
 
hunger,
 
never
 
fully
 
satisfied,
 
never
 
strong
 
enough
 
to
 
break
 
free
 
entirely.
 
Many  believe  this  endless  starvation  has  made  them  crueler  than  they  were  during  their  reign.  
Prisoners,  criminals,  political  enemies,  debtors,  cultists,  and  volunteers  are  regularly  brought  
into
 
the
 
feeding
 
chambers
 
beneath
 
powerful
 
cities.
 
Some
 
are
 
sacrificed
 
unwillingly.
 
Others
 
enter
 
willingly
 
in
 
exchange
 
for
 
wealth,
 
status,
 
miracles,
 
or
 
ichor.
 
Entire
 
black
 
markets
 
exist
 
around
 
feeding
 
rights
 
and
 
divine
 
exposure.
 
Noble
 
houses
 
secretly
 
compete
 
for
 
access
 
to
 
stronger
 
concentrations
 
of
 
ichor
 
harvested
 
from
 
particular
 
Gods.
 
The  public  is  taught  that  the  prisons  protect  civilization.  
The  truth  is  that  civilization  has  merely  transformed  divine  predation  into  an  industry.  
The  Gods  themselves  appear  almost  human.  
At  first  glance,  they  resemble  impossibly  tall  and  unnervingly  beautiful  mortals  draped  in  layered  
robes,
 
dark
 
ceremonial
 
fabrics,
 
and
 
armor-like
 
organic
 
growths
 
that
 
seem
 
half-grown
 
from
 
their
 
flesh.
 
Their
 
proportions
 
are
 
subtly
 
wrong.
 
Their
 
limbs
 
are
 
fractionally
 
too
 
long.
 
Their
 
fingers
 
bend
 
with
 
unnatural
 
fluidity.
 
Their
 
eyes
 
linger
 
too
 
still.
 
Their
 
smiles
 
hold
 
expressions
 
a
 
heartbeat
 
too
 
late.
 
Every
 
feature
 
evokes
 
the
 
uncanny
 
sensation
 
of
 
something
 
attempting
 
to
 
imitate
 
humanity
 
without
 
fully
 
understanding
 
it.
 
Their  skin  possesses  a  pale  translucence  beneath  which  black  ichor  pulses  slowly  through  
vein-like
 
channels.
 
Their
 
voices
 
sound
 
layered,
 
as
 
though
 
several
 
tones
 
speak
 
at
 
once
 
beneath
 
the
 
surface
 
of
 
ordinary
 
speech.
 
Their
 
movements
 
are
 
smooth
 
enough
 
to
 
feel
 
predatory
 
even
 
while
 
standing
 
motionless.
 
--- PAGE 3 ---
To  devoted  followers,  the  Gods  usually  feed  through  their  hands.  
Embedded  within  each  palm  lies  a  feeding  organ  concealed  beneath  smooth  flesh.  When  
feeding
 
begins,
 
the
 
palm
 
opens
 
like
 
layered
 
petals,
 
revealing
 
black
 
tendrils
 
and
 
sensory
 
filaments
 
that
 
pierce
 
both
 
flesh
 
and
 
spirit
 
simultaneously.
 
The
 
God
 
presses
 
its
 
hand
 
against
 
the
 
chest,
 
throat,
 
face,
 
or
 
heart
 
of
 
the
 
victim
 
while
 
drawing
 
life
 
force
 
directly
 
from
 
the
 
soul
 
itself.
 
For  loyal  worshippers,  favored  servants,  and  willing  thralls,  this  process  is  intimate,  euphoric,  
and
 
strangely
 
gentle
 
despite
 
the
 
horror
 
of
 
it.
 
The
 
Gods
 
deliberately
 
preserve
 
beauty
 
in
 
these
 
moments
 
because
 
devotion
 
enriches
 
the
 
feeding
 
experience.
 
Their
 
followers
 
often
 
describe
 
feelings
 
of
 
warmth,
 
transcendence,
 
emotional
 
release,
 
and
 
overwhelming
 
closeness
 
during
 
feeding.
 
Many
 
become
 
addicted
 
after
 
only
 
a
 
single
 
encounter.
 
But  the  Gods  are  not  kind  creatures.  
When  feeding  upon  enemies,  strangers,  or  victims  they  wish  to  terrify,  they  abandon  the  illusion  
of
 
refinement
 
entirely.
 
Their  bodies  unfold  into  something  monstrous.  
Mouths  split  open  across  their  flesh  in  impossible  numbers.  Jaws  emerge  along  their  arms,  ribs,  
throats,
 
torsos,
 
and
 
faces.
 
Teeth
 
blossom
 
beneath
 
the
 
skin
 
like
 
flowers
 
of
 
bone.
 
Entire
 
sections
 
of
 
their
 
bodies
 
peel
 
apart
 
into
 
feeding
 
maws
 
lined
 
with
 
black
 
tendrils
 
and
 
wet
 
sensory
 
organs.
 
Their
 
limbs
 
distort
 
and
 
elongate
 
while
 
mouths
 
speak,
 
scream,
 
laugh,
 
and
 
feed
 
simultaneously.
 
Some  Gods  enjoy  horrifying  their  victims  before  feeding.  Others  consider  it  entertainment.  A  few  
see
 
it
 
as
 
honesty,
 
stripping
 
away
 
the
 
false
 
human
 
shape
 
to
 
remind
 
mortals
 
what
 
stands
 
before
 
them.
 
The  prisons  suppress  much  of  this  transformation,  but  never  fully.  
Those  who  work  within  the  feeding  chambers  often  report  hearing  wet  breathing  behind  walls,  
whispers
 
emerging
 
from
 
sealed
 
stone,
 
or
 
the
 
sound
 
of
 
mouths
 
chewing
 
despite
 
no
 
visible
 
source.
 
The  ichor  produced  by  the  Gods  is  among  the  most  coveted  substances  in  existence.  
Fresh  ichor  appears  as  thick  black  fluid  with  an  oily  sheen  that  reflects  light  incorrectly.  It  
remains
 
warm
 
regardless
 
of
 
temperature
 
and
 
moves
 
slightly
 
on
 
its
 
own
 
when
 
exposed
 
to
 
living
 
flesh.
 
Consuming
 
it
 
can
 
produce
 
visions,
 
magical
 
enhancement,
 
heightened
 
perception,
 
prophetic
 
dreams,
 
or
 
horrifying
 
mutations
 
depending
 
upon
 
purity
 
and
 
dosage.
 
Dried  ichor  becomes  a  fine  black  powder  used  in  countless  applications:  
●  Spell  catalysts  and  magical  amplifiers  ●  Necromantic  rituals  
--- PAGE 4 ---
●  Healing  tonics  and  regenerative  medicine  ●  Addictive  narcotics  ●  Ritual  poisons  ●  Cursecraft  ●  Longevity  treatments  ●  Divine  communion  rites  ●  Psychic  enhancement  ●  Biological  experimentation  
The  most  powerful  courts  guard  their  ichor  reserves  more  fiercely  than  gold.  
Entire  wars  have  nearly  begun  over  access  to  imprisoned  Gods.  
Despite  their  imprisonment,  the  Gods  remain  dangerously  influential.  Their  whispers  still  reach  
beyond
 
their
 
prisons
 
through
 
dreams,
 
visions,
 
cults,
 
and
 
psychic
 
resonance.
 
Some
 
maintain
 
devoted
 
followings
 
numbering
 
in
 
the
 
thousands.
 
Others
 
secretly
 
manipulate
 
noble
 
bloodlines
 
or
 
criminal
 
organizations
 
from
 
within
 
confinement.
 
A
 
few
 
appear
 
genuinely
 
amused
 
by
 
the
 
current
 
arrangement,
 
seeing
 
mortals’
 
attempts
 
at
 
control
 
as
 
merely
 
another
 
stage
 
in
 
the
 
relationship
 
between
 
predator
 
and
 
prey.
 
The  world  of  Belavadös  now  exists  in  an  uneasy  balance.  
Civilization  fears  the  Gods.  
Civilization  depends  upon  the  Gods.  
And  beneath  the  great  cities  of  the  world,  the  creators  of  every  living  race  remain  hungry,  
half-starved,
 
and
 
very
 
much
 
alive.
 
 
The  Gods  and  Their  Created  Peoples  
1.  Nebyrr  
Creator  of  civilization,  lineage,  dynasties,  marriage,  inheritance,  and  social  order.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Human  ●  Half-Elf  ●  Half-Orc  ●  Khoravar  
--- PAGE 5 ---
●  Umbral  Human  
Nebyrr  appears  as  a  towering  noble  figure  with  soft  features  that  never  blink  at  the  correct  time.  
Their
 
skin
 
resembles
 
polished
 
ivory
 
stretched
 
over
 
moving
 
shadows.
 
Their
 
hands
 
are
 
delicate
 
and
 
warm
 
during
 
feeding,
 
often
 
cradling
 
the
 
face
 
or
 
chest
 
of
 
favored
 
followers
 
like
 
a
 
lover
 
or
 
parent.
 
 
2.  Sigrananna  
Mistress  of  forests,  moonlight,  wilderness,  graceful  hunters,  and  elven  beauty.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Elf  ●  Astral  Elf  ●  Eladrin  ●  Sea  Elf  ●  Shadowmoor  Elf  ●  Shadar-Kai  
Sigrananna  appears  almost  ethereal,  with  silver-black  hair  flowing  as  though  underwater.  Their  
smile
 
reveals
 
too
 
many
 
teeth
 
when
 
observed
 
closely.
 
 
3.  Marduthor  
Divine  smith  of  invention,  metalwork,  architecture,  and  subterranean  empires.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Dwarf  ●  Duergar  ●  Gnome  ●  Deep  Gnome  ●  Autognome  
Marduthor’s  body  carries  visible  seams  beneath  the  skin  like  forged  metal  plates  fitted  together  
imperfectly.
 
 
--- PAGE 6 ---
4.  Ishtanora  
Patron  of  hearths,  harvests,  villages,  fertility,  and  peaceful  rural  life.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Halfling  ●  Kender  ●  Kithkin  ●  Qickstep  ●  Hedge  ●  Jerbeen  
Ishtanora  appears  comforting  at  first  glance,  radiating  maternal  warmth  that  conceals  profound  
hunger.
 
 
5.  Enkirael  
God  of  conquest,  fury,  survival,  dominance,  and  warfare.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Orc  ●  Goblin  ●  Hobgoblin  ●  Bugbear  ●  Gnoll  ●  Gobboc  ●  Shadow  Goblin  
Enkirael’s  humanoid  form  constantly  flexes  as  though  restraining  violence  beneath  the  skin.  
 
6.  Anubaldir  
Titan  of  endurance,  burden,  colossal  strength,  and  primal  resilience.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Goliath  ●  Firbolg  
--- PAGE 7 ---
●  Cyclopian  ●  Giff  ●  Minotaur  
Anubaldir  resembles  a  massive  weathered  giant  whose  flesh  cracks  subtly  like  stone  under  
pressure.
 
 
7.  Valkhamesh  
Creator  of  draconic  bloodlines,  sovereignty,  storms,  and  ancient  dominion.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Dragonborn  ●  Kobold  ●  Lizardfolk  ●  Yuan-Ti  
Valkhamesh  speaks  with  a  low  vibrating  resonance  that  causes  discomfort  in  the  bones  of  
nearby
 
mortals.
 
 
8.  Freyseth  
Mistress  of  crossroads,  curses,  planar  bloodlines,  temptation,  and  forbidden  pacts.  
Created  Peoples  
●  Aasimar  ●  Tiefling  ●  Feral  Tiefling  ●  Hexblood  ●  Accursed  ●  Curseborn  
Freyseth’s  appearance  shifts  subtly  depending  upon  the  desires  and  fears  of  the  observer.  
 
9.  Nefarokir  
--- PAGE 8 ---
Lord  of  death,  spirits,  memory,  burial,  and  the  forgotten  dead.  
Created  Peoples:   
●  Dhampir  ●  Darakhul  ●  Reborn  ●  Arisen  ●  Graveborn  ●  Shade  ●  Downcast  
Nefarokir  is  one  of  the  High  Fathers  of  Belavadös,  an  imprisoned  divine  architect  who  governs  
death
 
not
 
as
 
cruelty,
 
judgment,
 
or
 
mercy,
 
but
 
as
 
inevitability
 
structured
 
into
 
reality
 
itself.
 
Within
 
his
 
dominion,
 
death
 
is
 
not
 
an
 
emotional
 
event
 
or
 
spiritual
 
punishment.
 
It
 
is
 
administration.
 
Every
 
living
 
creature
 
is
 
born
 
with
 
an
 
appointed
 
death
 
already
 
written
 
into
 
existence
 
through
 
a
 
cosmic
 
sequence
 
known
 
as
 
the
 
Design.
 
Every
 
soul
 
is
 
catalogued,
 
every
 
lifespan
 
measured,
 
and
 
every
 
ending
 
accounted
 
for
 
within
 
an
 
immense
 
metaphysical
 
ledger
 
that
 
sustains
 
the
 
divine
 
cycle.
 
Prayer,
 
grief,
 
fear,
 
devotion,
 
despair,
 
and
 
dying
 
itself
 
generate
 
spiritual
 
essence
 
consumed
 
by
 
the
 
imprisoned
 
gods,
 
and
 
Nefarokir
 
oversees
 
the
 
collection
 
and
 
redistribution
 
of
 
that
 
essence
 
with
 
absolute
 
precision.
 
To
 
him,
 
death
 
is
 
completion.
 
Survival
 
beyond
 
one’s
 
destined
 
end
 
is
 
an
 
error
 
within
 
the
 
system,
 
and
 
all
 
errors
 
must
 
eventually
 
be
 
corrected.
 
Before  the  Age  of  Containment,  Nefarokir  ruled  the  underworld  not  as  a  distant  kingdom  
beneath
 
the
 
earth,
 
but
 
as
 
a
 
distributed
 
mechanism
 
woven
 
directly
 
into
 
civilization.
 
In
 
Belavadös,
 
the
 
underworld
 
exists
 
through
 
infrastructure.
 
Brass
 
cathedrals,
 
rail
 
systems,
 
pressure
 
driven
 
foundries,
 
steamworks,
 
portal
 
regulators,
 
industrial
 
furnaces,
 
and
 
clock
 
towers
 
unknowingly
 
function
 
as
 
extensions
 
of
 
divine
 
administration.
 
Entire
 
cities
 
act
 
as
 
components
 
within
 
a
 
hidden
 
metaphysical
 
machine
 
designed
 
to
 
process
 
mortality.
 
Steam
 
lines
 
resemble
 
veins
 
carrying
 
soul
 
pressure
 
beneath
 
the
 
streets,
 
factory
 
whistles
 
echo
 
like
 
funeral
 
rites,
 
and
 
the
 
industrial
 
age
 
itself
 
became
 
a
 
physical
 
reflection
 
of
 
Nefarokir’s
 
Design.
 
Civilization
 
unknowingly
 
industrialized
 
death.
 
When  someone  escapes  their  destined  death  through  luck,  prophecy,  intervention,  or  magic,  
Nefarokir
 
rarely
 
acts
 
directly.
 
Instead,
 
reality
 
itself
 
begins
 
correcting
 
the
 
imbalance
 
with
 
terrifying
 
precision.
 
Mechanical
 
failures
 
synchronize.
 
Pressure
 
systems
 
rupture
 
at
 
impossible
 
moments.
 
Rusted
 
bolts
 
fail
 
beneath
 
exhausted
 
workers.
 
Bridges
 
collapse.
 
Boilers
 
explode.
 
Elevators
 
snap.
 
Oil
 
ignites.
 
Horses
 
panic
 
beside
 
crowded
 
streets.
 
At
 
first
 
these
 
incidents
 
appear
 
accidental,
 
but
 
patterns
 
quickly
 
emerge.
 
The
 
Design
 
behaves
 
as
 
an
 
intelligent
 
and
 
relentless
 
force
 
that
 
hunts
 
survivors
 
through
 
inevitability
 
itself.
 
Those
 
who
 
escape
 
death
 
become
 
unresolved
 
entries
 
within
 
the
 
cosmic
 
ledger,
 
and
 
Nefarokir
 
ensures
 
they
 
understand
 
they
 
have
 
been
 
found.
 
The
 
deaths
 
that
 
follow
 
are
 
often
 
bizarre,
 
elaborate,
 
and
 
unmistakably
 
intentional.
 
Only  two  known  methods  allow  mortals  to  meaningfully  evade  the  Design.  The  first  is  murder  
through
 
the
 
theft
 
of
 
years.
 
By
 
intentionally
 
killing
 
another
 
living
 
being,
 
a
 
person
 
may
 
steal
 
the
 
remaining
 
lifespan
 
that
 
fate
 
had
 
assigned
 
to
 
the
 
victim.
 
This
 
process,
 
known
 
as
 
taking
 
Borrowed
 
--- PAGE 9 ---
Years,  temporarily  shifts  Nefarokir’s  attention  because  another  death  has  filled  the  missing  place  
within
 
the
 
ledger.
 
However,
 
stolen
 
years
 
corrupt
 
the
 
soul.
 
Those
 
carrying
 
Borrowed
 
Years
 
begin
 
dreaming
 
from
 
the
 
victim’s
 
perspective,
 
hearing
 
the
 
dead
 
whisper
 
in
 
crowded
 
rooms,
 
and
 
watching
 
their
 
own
 
reflection
 
subtly
 
resemble
 
the
 
slain.
 
Clerics
 
sense
 
contamination
 
within
 
them,
 
animals
 
recoil
 
in
 
fear,
 
and
 
resurrection
 
magic
 
behaves
 
unpredictably
 
around
 
their
 
presence.
 
Murder
 
becomes
 
both
 
survival
 
mechanism
 
and
 
spiritual
 
corruption,
 
forcing
 
many
 
into
 
horrifying
 
moral
 
compromises
 
simply
 
to
 
remain
 
alive.
 
The  second  method  is  true  death  followed  by  resurrection.  When  a  soul  fully  crosses  into  death  
before
 
being
 
restored
 
through
 
powerful
 
resurrection
 
magic,
 
its
 
entry
 
briefly
 
disappears
 
from
 
the
 
cosmic
 
ledger.
 
During
 
this
 
interval,
 
Nefarokir
 
temporarily
 
loses
 
track
 
of
 
the
 
soul,
 
making
 
the
 
resurrected
 
individual
 
difficult
 
to
 
locate
 
for
 
a
 
time.
 
Ordinary
 
healing
 
magic
 
cannot
 
accomplish
 
this
 
because
 
it
 
merely
 
repairs
 
the
 
body
 
while
 
leaving
 
the
 
soul
 
marked
 
for
 
collection.
 
True
 
resurrection
 
interrupts
 
the
 
sequence
 
itself.
 
Those
 
restored
 
through
 
spells
 
such
 
as
 
Revivify,
 
Raise
 
Dead,
 
or
 
Resurrection
 
always
 
return
 
changed.
 
Their
 
flesh
 
remains
 
unnaturally
 
cold,
 
food
 
tastes
 
like
 
ash,
 
mirrors
 
distort
 
around
 
them,
 
and
 
the
 
scent
 
of
 
grave
 
soil
 
follows
 
them
 
during
 
rainfall.
 
These
 
lingering
 
alterations
 
are
 
known
 
as
 
Marks
 
of
 
Return,
 
reminders
 
that
 
resurrection
 
does
 
not
 
defeat
 
death
 
but
 
merely
 
confuses
 
the
 
Design
 
temporarily.
 
Within  this  cosmology,  reaching  the  brink  of  death  means  attracting  Nefarokir’s  personal  
attention.
 
The
 
moment
 
someone
 
collapses
 
near
 
death,
 
reality
 
begins
 
reacting
 
around
 
them.
 
Candles
 
extinguish.
 
Clocks
 
stop
 
ticking.
 
Animals
 
panic.
 
Steam
 
pipes
 
shriek
 
without
 
cause.
 
Rain
 
begins
 
unexpectedly
 
and
 
nearby
 
machinery
 
malfunctions
 
as
 
invisible
 
pressure
 
settles
 
over
 
the
 
scene.
 
Death
 
saving
 
throws
 
are
 
not
 
simple
 
game
 
mechanics
 
but
 
moments
 
where
 
the
 
soul
 
physically
 
resists
 
collection.
 
A
 
failed
 
death
 
save
 
feels
 
like
 
drowning
 
beneath
 
black
 
water
 
while
 
distant
 
funeral
 
bells
 
echo
 
through
 
darkness.
 
A
 
successful
 
save
 
feels
 
unnatural,
 
stolen
 
from
 
a
 
fate
 
that
 
should
 
already
 
be
 
complete.
 
Those
 
who
 
briefly
 
cross
 
the
 
threshold
 
sometimes
 
return
 
carrying
 
fragmented
 
memories
 
of
 
the
 
underworld
 
or
 
prophetic
 
visions
 
of
 
future
 
deaths
 
they
 
were
 
never
 
meant
 
to
 
witness.
 
Although  imprisoned  alongside  the  other  gods,  Nefarokir’s  influence  was  never  destroyed.  His  
confinement
 
merely
 
restricted
 
his
 
direct
 
consumption
 
while
 
preserving
 
the
 
Design
 
itself.
 
Even
 
from
 
prison,
 
he
 
continues
 
operating
 
through
 
distributed
 
inevitability.
 
Steamworks,
 
rail
 
systems,
 
pressure
 
grids,
 
and
 
industrial
 
infrastructure
 
all
 
perpetuate
 
his
 
administration
 
unknowingly.
 
Entire
 
civilizations
 
participate
 
in
 
his
 
will
 
without
 
understanding
 
it.
 
Even
 
rebellion
 
against
 
death
 
ultimately
 
strengthens
 
his
 
dominion
 
because
 
resistance
 
itself
 
becomes
 
another
 
entry
 
within
 
the
 
ledger.
 
Other
 
imprisoned
 
gods
 
quietly
 
fear
 
what
 
would
 
happen
 
if
 
Nefarokir’s
 
Design
 
ever
 
collapsed
 
completely.
 
Without
 
him,
 
souls
 
would
 
remain
 
unprocessed,
 
divine
 
feeding
 
cycles
 
would
 
fail,
 
and
 
reality
 
itself
 
might
 
fracture
 
beneath
 
the
 
pressure
 
of
 
unaccounted
 
death.
 
Nefarokir  is  also  the  creator  and  patron  of  numerous  death  bound  peoples  that  emerged  from  
failures,
 
distortions,
 
or
 
continuations
 
within
 
the
 
Design.
 
The
 
Dhampir
 
exist
 
suspended
 
between
 
mortality
 
and
 
vampirism,
 
eternally
 
caught
 
between
 
life
 
and
 
collection.
 
The
 
Darakhul
 
are
 
subterranean
 
beings
 
rewritten
 
by
 
corrupted
 
death
 
systems
 
into
 
cycles
 
of
 
endless
 
hunger.
 
The
 
Reborn
 
are
 
souls
 
forcibly
 
reconstructed
 
after
 
catastrophic
 
accounting
 
failures
 
and
 
later
 
--- PAGE 10 ---
stabilized  into  sustainable  existence.  The  Arisen  are  corpses  returned  to  function  not  through  
resurrection,
 
but
 
through
 
administrative
 
continuation
 
of
 
unfinished
 
records.
 
Graveborn
 
emerge
 
from
 
concentrated
 
soul
 
pressure
 
surrounding
 
mass
 
death
 
events,
 
while
 
Shades
 
are
 
incomplete
 
souls
 
trapped
 
between
 
dissolution
 
and
 
existence.
 
The
 
Downcast
 
are
 
those
 
rejected
 
from
 
proper
 
passage
 
into
 
the
 
afterlife,
 
returned
 
to
 
the
 
world
 
fractured
 
and
 
spiritually
 
unstable.
 
None
 
of
 
these
 
peoples
 
are
 
considered
 
mistakes
 
by
 
Nefarokir.
 
They
 
are
 
simply
 
alternative
 
outcomes
 
within
 
the
 
system
 
of
 
death.
 
Nefarokir  remains  distinct  among  the  High  Fathers  because  he  does  not  seek  worship  in  the  
traditional
 
sense.
 
He
 
maintains
 
continuity.
 
He
 
ensures
 
that
 
no
 
soul
 
escapes
 
accounting
 
forever
 
and
 
that
 
every
 
deviation
 
eventually
 
returns
 
to
 
balance.
 
His
 
skin
 
feels
 
cold
 
enough
 
to
 
numb
 
flesh
 
upon
 
contact,
 
as
 
though
 
all
 
warmth
 
and
 
vitality
 
are
 
slowly
 
drained
 
away
 
simply
 
by
 
proximity
 
to
 
his
 
presence.
 
Even
 
other
 
gods
 
acknowledge
 
him
 
as
 
the
 
silent
 
guarantor
 
of
 
inevitability.
 
Alongside
 
Hathruna,
 
who
 
governs
 
cycles
 
of
 
restoration
 
and
 
dormancy
 
between
 
eras
 
of
 
divine
 
feeding,
 
Nefarokir
 
preserves
 
the
 
rhythm
 
of
 
collapse
 
and
 
renewal
 
that
 
sustains
 
both
 
mortals
 
and
 
gods
 
alike.
 
Ultimately,  Nefarokir  embodies  the  central  truth  of  Belavadös.  Nothing  is  truly  lost.  Everything  is  
processed.
 
Mortals,
 
civilizations,
 
memories,
 
grief,
 
spirits,
 
and
 
even
 
gods
 
themselves
 
are
 
absorbed
 
into
 
endless
 
cycles
 
of
 
correction,
 
consumption,
 
and
 
renewal.
 
Death
 
is
 
not
 
an
 
ending
 
within
 
his
 
domain.
 
It
 
is
 
an
 
ongoing
 
administrative
 
force
 
embedded
 
into
 
reality
 
itself,
 
ensuring
 
that
 
every
 
life,
 
no
 
matter
 
how
 
fractured
 
or
 
prolonged,
 
is
 
eventually
 
accounted
 
for
 
within
 
the
 
Design.
 
 
10.  Thalunesh  
Patron  of  dreamers,  tricksters,  shapeshifters,  wanderers,  and  fey  mischief.

●  Faerie  ●  Fairy  ●  Changeling  ●  Lorwyn  Changeling  ●  Satyr  ●  Dreamer  
Thalunesh’s  face  changes  expression  too  rapidly,  cycling  through  emotions  in  unsettling  flashes.

Horundar’s  body  constantly  leaks  vapor,  ash,  seawater,  or  dust  depending  upon  emotional  
state.

Raeshkul’s  eyes  contain  layered  pupils  that  rotate  independently.

Asethyr’s  laughter  sounds  joyful  until  it  continues  several  seconds  too  long.  
 
22.  Nephthysra

Nephthysra  appears  impossibly  beautiful  in  ways  that  become  deeply  unsettling  the  longer  one

Belavadös  Pantheon  
  
--- PAGE 16 ---
Thoryn-Rahek  
In  the  Belavadösian  interpretation  of  the  old  Colossus  cycle,  Thoryn-Rahek  is  not  remembered  
merely
 
as
 
a
 
predecessor
 
to
 
the
 
Belavadösian
 
gods
 
but
 
as
 
the
 
first
 
true
 
expression
 
of
 
divine
 
hunger
 
given
 
will,
 
form,
 
and
 
catastrophic
 
consequence.
 
Born
 
as
 
the
 
youngest
 
son
 
of
 
The
 
Heavens
 
and
 
The
 
Earth,
 
he
 
emerged
 
within
 
the
 
earliest
 
rupture
 
of
 
celestial
 
order,
 
a
 
moment
 
when
 
existence
 
itself
 
was
 
still
 
unrefined
 
and
 
power
 
flowed
 
without
 
hierarchy
 
or
 
restraint.
 
With
 
a
 
sickle
 
shaped
 
through
 
The
 
Earth’s
 
subversive
 
will,
 
Thoryn-Rahek
 
overthrew
 
The
 
Heavens
 
in
 
an
 
act
 
that
 
Belavadösian
 
scholars
 
interpret
 
not
 
as
 
simple
 
rebellion
 
but
 
as
 
the
 
first
 
failed
 
model
 
of
 
divine
 
succession,
 
where
 
authority
 
is
 
seized
 
through
 
violent
 
extraction
 
rather
 
than
 
structured
 
transition.
 
From
 
the
 
moment
 
he
 
assumed
 
dominion
 
over
 
the
 
Colossus
 
order,
 
Thoryn-Rahek
 
embodied
 
a
 
contradiction
 
that
 
would
 
define
 
every
 
catastrophe
 
that
 
followed:
 
overwhelming
 
ambition
 
fused
 
with
 
corrosive
 
fear
 
of
 
replacement.
 
His  rule  did  not  yet  understand  the  concept  of  controlled  sacrificial  hierarchy  that  would  later  
define
 
the
 
Belavadösian
 
systems.
 
Instead,
 
Thoryn-Rahek
 
embodied
 
unregulated
 
divine
 
consumption
 
turned
 
inward
 
upon
 
his
 
own
 
lineage.
 
When
 
prophecy
 
warned
 
that
 
he
 
would
 
be
 
overthrown
 
by
 
his
 
own
 
offspring,
 
he
 
responded
 
not
 
with
 
governance
 
or
 
adaptation
 
but
 
with
 
ingestion,
 
swallowing
 
each
 
of
 
his
 
children
 
at
 
birth
 
in
 
an
 
attempt
 
to
 
preserve
 
absolute
 
continuity
 
of
 
rule.
 
In
 
Belavadösian
 
metaphysical
 
terms,
 
this
 
act
 
represents
 
a
 
primitive
 
and
 
unstable
 
form
 
of
 
essence
 
absorption,
 
a
 
corrupted
 
precursor
 
to
 
later
 
divine
 
feeding
 
systems
 
used
 
by
 
the
 
gods.
 
Rather
 
than
 
refining
 
spiritual
 
energy
 
through
 
ritualized

Thoryn-Rahek
 
internalized
 
it
 
whole,
 
binding
 
the
 
latent
 
divinity
 
of
 
his
 
children
 
inside
 
himself
 
without
 
structure,
 
balance,
 
or
 
release.
 
The
 
result
 
was
 
not
 
immortality
 
but
 
internal
 
collapse
 
delayed
 
by
 
sheer
 
will.
 
This  consumption  did  not  remain  limited  to  his  offspring  alone.  In  the  earliest,  most  chaotic  
period
 
of
 
his
 
reign,
 
Thoryn-Rahek
 
is
 
also
 
attributed
 
with
 
the
 
creation
 
of
 
malformed
 
beings
 
born
 
from
 
primordial

failed

digestion,
 
and
 
the
 
unstable

of
 
Colossus

that
 
could
 
not
 
be
 
properly
 
contained
 
within
 
a
 
single
 
form.
 
These

became
 
the
 
first
 
aberrant
 
races
 
of
 
Belavadös
 
myth:
 
the
 
Thri-Kreen,
 
hyper-adaptive
 
insectoid

by
 
fractured

fluid
 
deepborn

formed
 
from
 
oceanic
 
residue
 
and
 
divine

Oozekin,
 
semi-sentient
 
accumulations
 
of
 
rejected
 
essence
 
and
 
formless
 
will;
 
the
 
Opteran,
 
winged
 
memory-eaters
 
that
 
drift
 
between
 
physical
 
and
 
metaphysical

and

Silkborn,
 
delicate
 
yet
 
predatory

emerged
 
from

residual

Each

is

not

recombined

new

often

Thoryn-Rahek’s  personality,  as  preserved  in  Belavadösian  myth,  reflects  this  unstable  
architecture.
 
He
 
is
 
described
 
as
 
a
 
being
 
of
 
immense
 
will
 
but
 
fractured
 
perception,
 
where
 
authority
 
is
 
constantly
 
undermined
 
by
 
paranoia
 
and
 
the
 
fear
 
of
 
succession.
 
Each
 
act
 
of
 
consumption
 
strengthens
 
him
 
outwardly,
 
yet

swallowed
 
divinity
 
accumulates
 
as

rather
 
than
 
integrated
 
power.
 
This
 
is
 
the
 
central
 
contradiction
 
of
 
--- PAGE 17 ---
Thoryn-Rahek:  he  discovers  that  gods  can  be  sustained  through  devouring  kin,  yet  lacks  the  
ritual
 
structure,
 
hierarchy,
 
or

to

His

The  eventual  survival  of  his  youngest  child,  hidden  by  Iskareth,  introduces  the  opposing

When

are

reassembled

In

Even  after  his  fall,  Thoryn-Rahek  remains  the  most  dangerous  surviving  presence  within  the

He

Valkhamesh

Across  Belavadös,  the  gods  eventually  develop  an  entire  civilization  built  upon  regulated  divine

Prayer,

the  gods  are  not  distant  benefactors  but  dependent  entities  sustained  by  controlled  extraction  of

Even

Centuries  later,  the  most  powerful  forces  of  Belavadös  unite  in  secret  to  construct  vast

Despite  imprisonment,  the  gods  remain  active  within  constrained  influence.  They  speak  through

Unlike

The  seals  that  bind  the  gods  are  not  purely  physical  constructs  but  spiritual  fractures  embedded

Every

the

to  contain:  that  hunger,  once  given  divine  form,  never  disappears,  it  only  learns  new  ways  to

Anubaldir  
Within  the  cosmology  of  Belavadös,  Anubaldir  is  not  merely  a  Colossus  of  strength  but  a  
foundational

within

Once

The

suspended
 
upon

Anubaldir  endures  this  burden  as  something  beyond  suffering.  His  punishment  is  not  chains  or

existence
 
as

Unlike

Oskar'enlil  
In  the  mythic  framework  of  Belavadös,  the  gods  were  never  simply  divine  rulers,  but  engines  of

Across  Belavadös,  even  the  imprisoned  gods  remain  bound  to  the  frameworks  she  created.

Setrimir  
Before  the  rail  empires  unified  Belavadös  and  before  skyships  cut  luminous  paths  through  
stormlit
 
skies,
 
the
 
world
 
was
 
governed
 
openly
 
by
 
the
 
Belavadösian
 
pantheon,
 
divine

who
 
sustained

not

of
 
mortal

Every

from

was

into

Temples
 
functioned

clerics

Within  this  world  of  structured  divinity  and  controlled  suffering,  Setrimir  stands  apart  as  one  of

in

order
 
during

permitted

Valkhamesh  
In  Belavadösian  mythology,  the  Belavadösian  pantheon  once  ruled  the  continent  openly  as  
sovereign
 
gods
 
of
 
a
 
predatory
 
cosmos,
 
and
 
at
 
the

of
 
that

stands
 
Valkhamesh,
 
the

Father
 
of
 
Heaven,
 
whose

is

from
 
conquest,

and

of

order.
 
Across

Belavadös,

empires,

reshaped

were
 
not

of

but

rulers
 
who
 
sustained
 
immortality
 
through
 
the
 
extraction
 
of
 
mortal

Every

and

fed
 
upward
 
into
 
the
 
divine

sat

as

and

He

as

of

and

order,
 
yet

that
 
defines
 
both

and
 
the

of

world
 
beneath

Valkhamesh  was  born  during  the  violent  age  of  the  Colossuss  as  the  youngest  son  of

and

Even

Like  all  gods  of  Belavadös,  Valkhamesh  exists  within  a  wider  metaphysical  economy  of  worship  
and

For

Centuries  ago,  mortals  across  Belavadös  united  in  secret  to  imprison  the  gods  within  sealed

Colossus-forged
 
materials,

itself.

Valkhamesh

influence

For

of

Despite  imprisonment,  Valkhamesh  remains  dangerously  active.  He  rages  within  his

Nefarokir  
In  the  cosmology  of  Belavadös,  Nefarokir  stands  not  as  a  simple  god  of  death  but  as  one  of  the  
High
 
Fathers
 
who
 
governs
 
inevitability
 
as
 
a
 
structured
 
system
 
of
 
divine
 
accounting.
 
Among
 
the
 
imprisoned
 
pantheon,
 
he
 
embodies
 
death
 
as
 
infrastructure
 
rather
 
than
 
judgment,
 
cruelty,
 
or
 
mercy.
 
His
 
domain
 
is
 
not
 
merely
 
the
 
underworld
 
but
 
the
 
entire
 
process
 
by
 
which
 
mortal
 
essence
 
is
 
measured,
 
collected,
 
and
 
redistributed
 
as
 
sustenance
 
for
 
divine
 
immortality.
 
In
 
this
 
reality,
 
the
 
gods
 
endure
 
through
 
the
 
controlled
 
consumption
 
of
 
mortal
 
vitality,
 
where
 
prayer,
 
devotion,
 
fear,
 
grief,
 
and
 
longing
 
are
 
converted
 
into
 
spiritual
 
essence
 
that
 
replenishes
 
divine
 
existence.
 
Nefarokir
 
does
 
not
 
devour
 
in
 
rage
 
or
 
hunger.
 
He
 
maintains
 
balance.
 
Every
 
life
 
that
 
ends
 
is
 
not
 
an
 
emotional
 
event
 
but
 
a
 
completed
 
transaction,
 
and
 
every
 
deviation
 
from
 
that
 
cycle
 
is
 
treated
 
as
 
a
 
structural
 
error
 
within
 
the
 
greater
 
divine
 
ledger.
 
Before  the  Age  of  Containment,  Nefarokir  ruled  the  underworld  as  a  distributed  mechanism  
rather
 
than
 
a
 
singular
 
realm.
 
In
 
Belavadös
 
interpretation,
 
the
 
underworld
 
is
 
embedded
 
within
 
the
 
industrial
 
world
 
itself,
 
expressed
 
through
 
vast
 
steampunk
 
systems
 
of
 
brass
 
cathedrals,
 
pressure-driven
 
cities,
 
rail
 
networks,
 
arcane
 
machinery,
 
and
 
clockwork
 
infrastructure
 
that
 
unknowingly
 
functions
 
as
 
an
 
extension
 
of
 
divine
 
administration.
 
Steam
 
lines,
 
piston
 
arrays,
 
maintenance
 
chutes,
 
and
 
ritualized
 
industrial
 
systems
 
act
 
as
 
the
 
physical
 
limbs
 
of
 
inevitability.
 
When
 
a
 
soul
 
fails
 
to
 
reach
 
its
 
appointed
 
end,
 
Nefarokir
 
does
 
not
 
intervene
 
directly.
 
Instead,
 
reality
 
itself
 
begins
 
to
 
correct
 
the

Environmental
 
systems
 
shift
 
with
 
impossible
 
precision,
 
accidents
 
align
 
into
 
inevitability,
 
and
 
seemingly
 
random
 
mechanical
 
failures
 
converge
 
into
 
outcomes
 
that
 
restore
 
the
 
correct
 
balance
 
of
 
death
 
and
 
release.

This  influence  extends  even  after  his  imprisonment  beneath  the  First  Seal.  Nefarokir  remains  
bound,

is

temporary

within

always

Nefarokir  remains  distinct  among  the  High  Fathers  because  he  does  not  compete  for  worship  or

periods  of  dormancy  and  restoration  between  eras  of  divine  feeding.  Together,  they  regulate  the

Horundar  
In  the  mythic  structure  of  Belavadös,  Horundar  is  not  merely  the  god  of  seas,  storms,  
earthquakes,
 
and
 
horses

a

Marduthor  
In  the  Belavadösian  pantheon,  the  gods  were  not  gentle  stewards  of  creation  but  vast  predatory  
intelligences
 
sustained
 
through
 
structured
 
consumption

Among

Enkirael

Within  the  Age  of  Containment,  Enkirael  is  both  weapon  and  warning.  Civilizations  depend  upon

In  the  broader  truth  of  Belavadös,  Enkirael  stands  as  the  purest  expression  of  divine  hunger

by

Sokhivar  
Within  the  Belavadösian  pantheon,  Sokhivar  stands  as  one  of  the  most  radiant  and  feared  
divine
 
aristocrats,
 
a
 
Belavadösian-tier
 
High
 
Father
 
whose
 
perfection
 
is
 
both
 
aesthetic
 
and
 
predatory,
 
embodying
 
symmetry
 
made
 
living
 
form
 
and
 
intelligence
 
sharpened
 
into
 
divine
 
law.
 
Born
 
of
 
Valkhamesh
 
and
 
Leto
 
and
 
twin
 
to
 
Sigrananna,

represent

Belavadös
 
ideal
 
of
 
structured
 
brilliance,
 
where
 
light
 
is
 
not
 
only
 
illumination

authority,
 
and

from

Their

of

mortal
 
essence
 
is

and

upward
 
into

sustaining

Sokhivar’s  prophetic  power  is  expressed  through  resonance  and  extraction  rather  than  simple  
foresight.

Through

to

futures
 
while

the

becomes
 
a
 
quiet
 
draining
 
of
 
emotional
 
and

Their

Thalunesh  
Within  the  Belavadösian  pantheon,  Thalunesh  stands  as  far  more  than  the  god  of  travel  and

Nebyrr

Freyseth  
Within  the  Belavadösian  pantheon,  Freyseth  stands  as  the  Liminal  Matron  of  Thresholds,  an  
ancient
 
divine
 
intelligence
 
whose
 
authority
 
predates
 
the
 
structured
 
hierarchy
 
of
 
the
 
gods
 
--- PAGE 53 ---
themselves.  She  governs  magic,  witchcraft,  crossroads,  ghosts,  forbidden  knowledge,  and  the  
fragile
 
boundaries
 
between
 
all
 
states
 
of
 
existence.
 
Unlike
 
deities

rose
 
through

or

of
 
celestial

transition
 
itself,
 
the

where
 
one

where

becomes
 
death,

mortal
 
essence
 
becomes
 
divine

Her

is

unstable

In  Belavadös  myth  she  is  the  silent  guardian  of  “thin  places,”  crossroads,  burial  grounds,  
doorways,
 
ritual

liminal

between

can

or
 
redirected
 
through
 
metaphysical

Her  appearance  reflects  the  divine  ideal  of  Belavadösian  cosmology,  statuesque,  radiant,  and  
unnervingly

Freyseth  is  not  counted  among  the  High  Fathers  of  Heaven  nor  fully  among  the  subordinate

Ishtanora  
Within  the  Belavadösian  pantheon,  Ishtanora  is  revered  as  far  more  than  a  nurturing  goddess  of

She

Hathruna  
In  Belavadös,  the  ancient  gods  are  not  distant  myths  but  imprisoned  systems  of  appetite  and  
control,
 
bound
 
within
 
metaphysical
 
vault-prisons
 
forged
 
four
 
centuries
 
ago
 
by
 
an
 
unprecedented
 
alliance
 
of
 
industrial
 
powers,
 
arcane
 
orders,
 
monarchies,
 
and
 
revolutionary
 
movements.
 
The
 
Belavadösian
 
pantheon
 
once
 
ruled
 
openly
 
across
 
the
 
continent,
 
sustained
 
not
 
only
 
by
 
worship
 
but
 
by
 
the
 
extraction
 
of
 
mortal
 
essence
 
through
 
prayer,
 
sacrifice,
 
emotion,
 
and
 
suffering.
 
Valkhamesh
 
governed
 
storm
 
and
 
law
 
from
 
celestial
 
courts,
 
Horundar
 
ruled
 
the
 
depths,
 
Nefarokir
 
presided
 
over
 
the
 
dead,
 
Raeshkul
 
shaped
 
strategy
 
and
 
empire,
 
Enkirael
 
fed
 
upon
 
war,
 
and
 
every
 
other
 
deity
 
embodied
 
a
 
distinct
 
form
 
of
 
consumption.
 
Civilizations
 
eventually
 
learned
 
that
 
divinity
 
was
 
not
 
symbolic
 
but
 
predatory,
 
and
 
that
 
faith
 
itself
 
was
 
a
 
resource
 
being
 
harvested.
 
This

led
 
to
 
the

of
 
twenty-one
 
seal-prisons
 
designed
 
not
 
to
 
kill
 
the
 
gods
 
but
 
to
 
restrict
 
their
 
ability
 
to
 
feed,
 
forcing
 
them
 
into
 
controlled
 
starvation
 
while
 
still
 
allowing
 
limited,
 
regulated
 
influence
 
over
 
mortal
 
civilization.
 
Even
 
in

gods
 
remain
 
active,
 
whispering
 
through
 
dreams,
 
sanctioned
 
rituals,
 
and
 
tightly
 
controlled
 
divine
 
systems
 
that
 
modern

still
 
depends
 
upon,
 
from
 
skyship
 
arcane

to
 
portal
 
stabilizers
 
and
 
consecrated
 
rail

Beneath

the
 
greater

of

first

imprisoned
 
deeper

is
 
foretold
 
to
 
collapse
 
the
 
fragile
 
balance
 
between
 
survival
 
and
 
annihilation.
 
Within  this  constrained  divine  ecology,  Hathruna  occupies  one  of  the  most  essential  and  
paradoxical
 
thrones.
 
She
 
is
 
not
 
merely
 
the

of
 
seasonal

the
 
structured
 
axis
 
between
 
growth
 
and

ascent

Born

unclaimed

Belavadösian

unallocated

Her

into

not

The

The

regulated

and

Through

From  Hathruna’s  influence  emerge  several  foundational  post-divine  ecological  races  shaped  by

Bastveig  
Before  the  rail  empires  spread  across  Belavadös  and  skyships  carved  their  regulated  paths  
through
 
stormlit
 
industry,
 
the
 
world
 
was
 
ruled
 
directly
 
by
 
gods
 
whose

was
 
sustained
 
not
 
by

but
 
by
 
consumption.
 
The

held

over
 
storm,

harvest,

and
 
countless

each
 
deity
 
feeding
 
upon
 
mortal

drawn
 
from
 
prayer,
 
sacrifice,
 
emotion,
 
and
 
suffering.
 
Civilization

merely
 
worship

nourished
 
them,
 
and
 
as

into
 
industrial

Wars,

Over  centuries,  humanity  discovered  the  truth  beneath  organized  faith,  that  the  gods  could

Sigrananna  
Within  the  Belavadösian  interpretation  of  the  pantheon,  Sigrananna  is  not  merely  the  goddess  of  
the
 
hunt
 
and
 
wilderness,
 
but
 
a
 
celestial
 
predator
 
bound
 
to
 
the
 
sacred
 
cycle
 
of
 
vitality
 
and
 
depletion.
 
She
 
embodies
 
untamed
 
nature
 
in
 
its
 
most
 
merciless
 
expression,
 
where
 
forests
 
do
 
not
 
simply
 
shelter
 
life
 
but
 
consume
 
and
 
reclaim
 
it
 
in
 
equal
 
measure.
 
As
 
a
 
twin
 
of
 
Sokhivar
 
and
 
daughter
 
of
 
Valkhamesh
 
and
 
Leto,
 
she
 
occupies
 
a
 
liminal
 
position
 
within
 
divine
 
aristocracy,
 
respected
 
and
 
feared
 
in
 
equal
 
measure,
 
never
 
fully
 
controlled
 
even
 
by
 
the
 
High
 
Fathers
 
of
 
Heaven.
 
Among
 
all
 
divine
 
forces,
 
she
 
is
 
regarded
 
as
 
the
 
most
 
precise
 
embodiment
 
of
 
natural
 
law
 
made
 
sentient,
 
governing
 
thresholds
 
where
 
life
 
begins,
 
where
 
it
 
is
 
taken,
 
and
 
where
 
it
 
returns
 
to
 
the
 
wild
 
order
 
that
 
predates
 
civilization.
 
Sigrananna’s  domain  extends  across  wilderness,  moonlight,  animals,  and  childbirth,  all  of  which  
reflect
 
controlled
 
transitions
 
rather
 
than
 
static
 
states
 
of
 
existence.
 
In
 
Belavadös
 
myth,
 
her
 
famed
 
independence
 
is
 
understood
 
not
 
as
 
simple

of
 
divine

but
 
as

of
 
being
 
bound
 
to

of

pantheon.
 
She
 
does
 
not

through

Her

are

and

always

as

of

than

To

neither

an

that

and
 
when

to

of
 
the

From  Sigrananna  are  born  the  elven  peoples  of  Belavadös,  each  reflecting  a  different

The  Sea  Elf  are  born  from  her  reach  into  tidal  wilderness,  where  ocean  and  shoreline  become

between

sensitivity,  their  existence  often  destabilizing  rigid  emotional  environments  simply  by  being  
witnessed.
 
The
 
Luma
 
carry
 
an
 
almost
 
luminous
 
charisma
 
that
 
distorts
 
perception
 
and
 
softens
 
hostility,
 
creating
 
spaces
 
where
 
desire,
 
trust,
 
and

emerge

These

are
 
not
 
accidental
 
byproducts
 
of
 
culture
 
but
 
deliberate
 
reflections
 
of
 
Nephthysra’s
 
dominion,
 
living
 
embodiments
 
of
 
desire
 
translated
 
into
 
civilization.
 
Within  the  wider  divine  structure  of  Belavadös,  Nephthysra  stands  alongside  gods  who  each  
embody
 
different
 
forms
 
of
 
consumption.
 
Valkhamesh
 
governs
 
authority
 
and
 
storm-driven
 
dominion,
 
Horundar
 
consumes
 
through
 
oceanic
 
vastness,
 
Nefarokir
 
through
 
inevitability
 
of
 
death,
 
Raeshkul
 
through
 
calculated
 
order,
 
and
 
Enkirael
 
through
 
violence
 
and
 
fear.
 
Nephthysra’s
 
consumption
 
is
 
uniquely
 
subtle
 
because
 
it
 
operates
 
through
 
consent
 
shaped
 
by
 
longing
 
rather
 
than
 
coercion.
 
This
 
places
 
them
 
in
 
frequent
 
conceptual
 
opposition
 
to
 
Raeshkul
 
and
 
Sigrananna,
 
who
 
resist
 
emotional
 
manipulation
 
as
 
a
 
system
 
of
 
control,
 
embodying
 
discipline,
 
distance,
 
and
 
refusal
 
in
 
contrast
 
to
 
Nephthysra’s
 
relational
 
inevitability.
 
The  world  itself  exists  under  the  shadow  of  a  far  older  hunger.  Beneath  all  creation  lies  
Thoryn-Rahek,
 
the
 
Father
 
of
 
Monsters,
 
imprisoned
 
after
 
the
 
Colossus
 
Wars
 
that
 
nearly
 
erased
 
existence
 
itself.
 
In
 
that
 
ancient
 
conflict,
 
entire
 
civilizations
 
were
 
consumed
 
by
 
primordial
 
devastation,
 
forcing
 
the
 
surviving
 
gods
 
into
 
a
 
state
 
of
 
fear
 
that
 
reshaped
 
their
 
nature
 
into
 
something
 
dependent
 
on
 
mortal
 
essence.
 
From
 
this
 
fear
 
emerged
 
a
 
divine
 
economy
 
where
 
worship
 
became
 
sustenance
 
and
 
suffering
 
became
 
currency.
 
Every
 
prayer,
 
sacrifice,
 
and
 
act
 
of
 
devotion
 
carries
 
fragments
 
of
 
mortal
 
essence
 
upward
 
into
 
the
 
divine
 
realm,
 
feeding
 
gods
 
who
 
can
 
no
 
longer
 
exist
 
without
 
it.
 
As  civilizations  across  Belavadös  advanced  into  industrial  and  arcane  ages,  humanity  
discovered
 
that
 
the
 
gods
 
were
 
not
 
distant
 
observers
 
but
 
systems
 
of
 
consumption
 
embedded
 
into
 
reality
 
itself.
 
Rail
 
empires,
 
skyships,
 
and
 
regulated
 
portal
 
networks
 
now
 
function
 
on
 
controlled
 
divine
 
remnants,
 
binding
 
civilization
 
to
 
the
 
very
 
forces
 
it
 
once
 
sought
 
to
 
escape.
 
The
 
gods
 
were
 
eventually
 
imprisoned
 
through
 
coordinated

between
 
industrial
 
powers,
 
arcane

monarchies,
 
and

in

of
 
twenty-one
 
metaphysical

designed
 
not
 
to

but
 
to
 
restrict

to
 
feed.
 
These

form
 
a

architecture
 
of
 
containment,
 
enforced
 
through
 
suffering
 
feedback
 
loops
 
that
 
punish
 
excessive
 
divine
 
consumption
 
with
 
unbearable
 
metaphysical

Despite  their  imprisonment,  the  gods  remain  active  within  constrained  limits,  able  to  hear  
prayers,
 
grant
 
carefully
 
regulated
 
blessings,
 
and
 
influence
 
mortal
 
dreams.
 
Nephthysra
 
continues
 
to
 
operate
 
through
 
controlled
 
channels
 
of
 
desire,
 
shaping
 
emotional

even
 
under
 
restriction,
 
while

manipulate

in

Yet

that
 
binds

is
 
unstable,
 
because
 
the
 
seals
 
weaken
 
through
 
accumulated
 
human

corruption,
 
and
 
emotional

Civilization
 
itself
 
becomes
 
both
 
prison
 
and
 
potential
 
release
 
mechanism,
 
as
 
every
 
act
 
of
 
despair
 
or
 
moral
 
fracture
 
chips
 
away
 
at
 
the
 
structure
 
holding
 
divinity
 
in
 
check.
 
Nephthysra’s  influence  in  this  imprisoned  age  is  especially  dangerous  because  desire  cannot  be  
fully
 
regulated
 
without
 
collapsing
 
into
 
deprivation.
 
Even
 
under
 
restriction,
 
they
 
continue
 
to
 
shape

relationships,  politics,  and  cultural  systems  through  subtle  emotional  dependency.  Their  created

The  broader  theology  of  Belavadös  recognizes  Nephthysra  as  one  of  the  most  elegant

Eirzunet  
Before  the  rise  of  rail  empires,  skyships,  and  regulated  portal  networks  across  Belavadös,  the  
world
 
existed
 
under
 
open
 
divine
 
rule,
 
where
 
the
 
gods
 
were
 
not
 
distant
 
symbols
 
of
 
worship
 
but
 
active
 
sovereign
 
predators
 
sustained
 
by
 
mortal
 
devotion.
 
Every
 
prayer,
 
sacrifice,
 
and
 
act
 
of
 
faith
 
fed
 
them,
 
not
 
metaphorically
 
but
 
as
 
literal
 
transfers
 
of
 
essence.
 
Civilization
 
grew
 
around
 
this
 
reality,
 
interpreting
 
divine
 
consumption
 
as
 
sacred
 
balance,
 
while
 
unknowingly
 
reinforcing
 
a
 
system
 
built
 
on
 
spiritual
 
extraction.
 
Even
 
after
 
centuries
 
of
 
advancement,
 
that
 
foundational
 
truth
 
never
 
changed:
 
the
 
gods
 
did
 
not
 
simply
 
rule
 
reality,
 
they
 
fed
 
upon
 
it.
 
Within  this  divine  ecosystem  exists  Eirzunet,  reinterpreted  in  Belavadös  myth  as  a  demi-female  
lesser
 
divinity-adjacent
 
sorceress
 
and
 
exiled
 
practitioner
 
of
 
divine-grade
 
thaumaturgy.
 
Eirzunet
 
stands
 
at
 
the
 
threshold
 
between
 
mortal
 
witchcraft
 
and
 
predatory
 
divine
 
hunger,
 
neither
 
fully
 
goddess
 
nor
 
mortal,
 
but
 
something
 
deliberately
 
self-fashioned
 
in
 
isolation.
 
Her
 
domain,
 
the
 
island
 
of
 
Aeaea,
 
is
 
not
 
merely
 
a
 
remote
 
sanctuary
 
but
 
a
 
controlled
 
feeding
 
ground
 
disguised
 
as
 
refuge,
 
where
 
hospitality
 
itself
 
is
 
weaponized
 
into
 
ritual.
 
Travelers
 
arrive
 
believing
 
they
 
have
 
found
 
safety,
 
only
 
to
 
step
 
into
 
a
 
carefully
 
constructed
 
metaphysical
 
system
 
designed
 
to
 
destabilize
 
identity
 
and
 
render
 
the
 
soul
 
malleable.
 
Aeaea  operates  as  both  temple  and  processing  ground.  Guests  are  offered  enchanted  food  and  
drink
 
that
 
function
 
as
 
preparatory
 
rites,
 
softening
 
resistance
 
and
 
loosening
 
the
 
structure
 
of
 
the
 
mortal
 
spirit.
 
Once
 
altered,
 
victims
 
are
 
reshaped
 
through
 
transformation
 
magic,
 
most
 
often
 
into
 
beasts
 
such
 
as
 
swine,
 
lions,
 
wolves,
 
and
 
other
 
forms
 
designed
 
not
 
as
 
punishment
 
alone
 
but
 
as
 
functional
 
reconfiguration.
 
In
 
Belavadös
 
cosmology,
 
these
 
transformations
 
represent
 
the
 
conversion
 
of
 
complex
 
mortal
 
essence
 
into
 
simpler,
 
more
 
digestible
 
states
 
of
 
vitality,
 
allowing
 
Eirzunet
 
to
 
metabolize
 
identity
 
itself
 
over
 
time.
 
--- PAGE 68 ---
Her  predation  does  not  mirror  the  overwhelming  consumption  practiced  by  the  High  Fathers  of  
the
 
pantheon.
 
Instead,
 
Eirzunet’s
 
hunger
 
is
 
intimate
 
and
 
prolonged.
 
She
 
siphons
 
essence
 
gradually
 
through
 
enchantment,
 
emotional
 
dependency,
 
and
 
magical
 
proximity,
 
binding
 
particularly
 
resilient
 
individuals
 
as
 
long-term
 
consorts
 
or
 
companions.
 
Over
 
time,
 
these
 
individuals
 
become
 
hollow
 
reflections
 
of
 
themselves,
 
sustained
 
just
 
enough
 
to
 
remain
 
coherent
 
while
 
their
 
vitality
 
is
 
continuously
 
drawn
 
away.
 
This
 
cycle
 
creates
 
a
 
disturbing
 
rhythm
 
of
 
depletion
 
and
 
partial
 
restoration,
 
as
 
drained
 
remnants
 
are
 
sometimes
 
returned
 
to
 
the
 
world
 
altered,
 
disoriented,
 
and
 
inexplicably
 
drawn
 
back
 
to
 
Aeaea
 
as
 
if
 
bound
 
by
 
invisible
 
hunger.
 
The  rare  herb  moly,  traditionally  associated  with  Thalunesh,  is  reinterpreted  within  this  mythos  
as
 
a
 
stabilizing
 
counter-agent
 
against
 
divine
 
metabolic
 
influence,
 
one
 
of
 
the
 
few
 
known
 
substances
 
capable
 
of
 
resisting
 
Eirzunet’s
 
enchantments
 
long
 
enough
 
to
 
interrupt
 
her
 
feeding
 
cycles.
 
Those
 
who
 
carry
 
it
 
can
 
briefly
 
retain
 
coherence
 
of
 
self,
 
disrupting
 
transformation
 
and
 
resisting
 
the
 
emotional
 
and
 
magical
 
dependencies
 
that
 
sustain
 
her
 
domain.
 
Even
 
so,
 
such
 
resistance
 
is
 
temporary,
 
and
 
often
 
only
 
delays
 
the
 
inevitable
 
recalibration
 
of
 
her
 
influence.
 
Eirzunet  herself  embodies  contradiction.  She  is  highly  intelligent,  aesthetically  refined,  and  
profoundly
 
isolated,
 
yet
 
driven
 
by
 
a
 
compulsive
 
need
 
to
 
sustain
 
herself
 
through
 
stolen
 
vitality.
 
She
 
does
 
not
 
align
 
fully
 
with
 
the
 
ruling
 
pantheon
 
nor
 
oppose
 
it
 
outright,
 
instead
 
existing
 
as
 
a
 
liminal
 
imitation
 
of
 
divine
 
predation
 
scaled
 
down
 
into
 
personal,
 
ritualized
 
intimacy.
 
As
 
a
 
demi-female
 
entity,
 
Eirzunet
 
exists
 
outside
 
rigid
 
mortal
 
understandings
 
of
 
womanhood
 
and
 
divinity
 
alike,
 
embodying
 
an
 
intentionally
 
incomplete
 
and
 
fluid
 
femininity
 
that
 
shifts
 
between
 
maternal
 
allure,
 
predatory
 
elegance,
 
and
 
unnerving
 
androgyny.
 
Her
 
charisma
 
and
 
sensuality
 
are
 
not
 
mere
 
traits
 
but
 
structured
 
instruments
 
of
 
survival,
 
designed
 
to
 
maintain
 
proximity
 
between
 
herself
 
and
 
those
 
she
 
feeds
 
upon.
 
Those
 
who
 
escape
 
her
 
island
 
often
 
do
 
so
 
carrying
 
fragmented
 
knowledge
 
of
 
divine
 
systems,
 
speaking
 
in
 
warnings
 
about
 
cycles
 
of
 
consumption
 
and
 
the
 
broader
 
hunger
 
underlying
 
existence
 
itself.
 
From  Eirzunet’s  influence  emerge  several  twilight-adjacent  races,  each  representing  a  different  
mode
 
of
 
transformation
 
and
 
residual
 
identity
 
distortion.
 
The
 
Ombrask
 
are
 
beings
 
formed
 
from
 
prolonged
 
exposure
 
to
 
Aeaea’s
 
shadowed
 
enchantments,
 
their
 
bodies
 
stabilized
 
between
 
human
 
and
 
beast
 
states,
 
often
 
retaining
 
fractured
 
memories
 
of
 
their
 
original
 
selves
 
while
 
developing
 
heightened
 
nocturnal
 
perception
 
and
 
adaptive
 
camouflage
 
instincts.
 
The
 
Ruinbound
 
are
 
those
 
whose
 
transformations
 
collapsed
 
mid-process,
 
leaving
 
them
 
partially
 
restored
 
but
 
spiritually
 
hollow,
 
functioning
 
as
 
wandering
 
remnants
 
bound
 
to
 
decay
 
and
 
instability,
 
often
 
drawn
 
back
 
toward
 
sites
 
of
 
magical
 
saturation.
 
The
 
Blagueborn
 
originate
 
from
 
victims
 
whose
 
identities
 
were
 
reshaped
 
through
 
sustained
 
enchantment
 
loops,
 
resulting
 
in
 
beings
 
defined
 
by
 
compulsive
 
imitation,
 
fractured
 
emotion,
 
and
 
unstable
 
self-reinvention,
 
often
 
mirroring
 
those
 
around
 
them
 
in
 
unsettlingly
 
adaptive
 
ways.
 
The
 
Threadborn
 
are
 
the
 
most
 
metaphysically
 
altered,
 
individuals
 
whose
 
essence
 
was
 
not
 
fully
 
consumed
 
but
 
rewoven
 
into
 
lingering
 
magical
 
filaments,
 
granting
 
them
 
strange
 
perceptual
 
awareness
 
of
 
fate,
 
memory,
 
and
 
proximity
 
to
 
Eirzunet’s
 
influence,
 
as
 
if
 
their
 
existence
 
remains
 
partially
 
stitched
 
into
 
Aeaea’s
 
underlying
 
structure.
 
Across  all  of  these  creations,  Eirzunet’s  influence  reveals  a  smaller  reflection  of  the  broader  
Belavadösian
 
divine
 
condition.
 
The
 
gods
 
consume
 
faith,
 
emotion,
 
and
 
civilization
 
itself
 
on
 
a
 
--- PAGE 69 ---
continental  scale,  while  Eirzunet  reproduces  the  same  principle  in  localized,  intimate  form.  Her  
transformations,
 
her
 
enchantments,
 
and
 
her
 
creations
 
all
 
demonstrate
 
the

central

that
 
defines
 
the

nothing

without

and

a

of
